  The re-run delay — couldn't reproduce, but I think I know what you saw

  Two probes now assert the badge flips within 200 ms of clicking Re-run: one
  with the PATCH stalled 3 seconds, one with a list refetch also stalled and in
  flight. Both pass. So the optimistic write isn't queued behind anything, and
  my await cancelQueries theory was wrong.

  What you most likely saw is a design decision working as intended, and looking
  wrong on slow hardware. From slice-7 review finding #4, the row deliberately 
  stays busy until the reconciling refetch lands — that's what stops stale
  allowed_transitions from being actionable. So:

  - the badge flips immediately ✓
  - but the "saving…" hint and the disabled Re-run button persist until the
  refetch completes

  That window scales with machine speed and table size — and the database had 
  250,000 rows in it at the time you were clicking around, from the latency
  measurement. If you were watching the row rather than the badge specifically,
  a lingering busy state would read exactly as "pending didn't show up right
  away."

  I've kept both probes as permanent regression coverage. They pin a property
  that's easy to break accidentally: optimism has to stay immediate.

  11 is just the cold gate, and you wanted a timing figure from a faster machine
  before anything about first-run duration goes in the README, which is the
  right call.

      161  Stored as strings, not ints — readable in the DB and in API pa
           yloads, and adding a state doesn't require a
     ...
      163  `(no-op)` for both columns, because `choices` is Django-level 
           metadata and the column is already
      164  `varchar(16)`.
      165  
      166 +The flip side showed up later, when the spelling changed from 
          +`CANCELLED` to `CANCELED`. *Adding* a state                   
      167 +is free; *renaming* one is not, and for exactly the reason the
          + column is cheap — the value is stored as a                   
      168 +string, so altering `choices` alone would have left every exis
          +ting row saying `CANCELLED` while the                         
      169 +application no longer recognised it. Migration `0003_canceled_
          +spelling` therefore carries a `RunPython`                     
      170 +step that respells both the log and the projection, and is rev
          +ersible in both directions. Readable string                   
      171 +enums trade a free schema for a data migration on rename; that
          + is the right trade here, but it is a trade.                  
      172 +                                                              
      173  **Canceling is not deleting.** Deleting removes the record; ca
           nceling stops the work and keeps it. A
      174  canceled job still consumed compute time, and that is precisel
           y the history worth auditing — so the two
      175  are different operations with different outcomes, not two rout
           es to the same end.

⏺ Rename is done — .slice( untouched, nothing stale left outside the transcripts
  (which stay as historical records).

  Three changes so far

  1. Delete dialog. The "cancel it instead" clause now only appears when
  canceling is actually reachable — read from the server's allowed_transitions,
  so the copy can't disagree with what the status control offers. Added "Are you
  sure you want to delete it?", and the buttons are "No, exit" / "Yes, delete 
  it". You were right that "Cancel" was confusing: in a dialog about a job that
  can itself be canceled, it reads as "cancel the job".

  New spec asserts both halves — a pending job gets the suggestion, a completed
  job doesn't, and both get the question. I also had to fix .dialog p → .dialog 
  p.first() in an existing test, since there are two paragraphs now.

  2. CANCELLED → CANCELED, including the wire value. This needed a real data
  migration, and it's the interesting part: choices is Django metadata, so
  altering it alone is a no-op against Postgres — every existing row would have
  kept saying CANCELLED while the app no longer recognized it.
  0003_canceled_spelling respells both the log and the projection, and is
  reversible.

  Verified on live data: 6 jobs and 8 log entries moved, both tables consistent,
  nothing left behind.

  One nice side effect — a backend test failed because the error message "Only 
  canceled or failed jobs can be re-run" is built from " or 
  ".join(sorted(RETRYABLE)).lower(). The message respelled itself; only the
  test's regex was stale. That's the message enumerating RETRYABLE doing its
  job.

  I also added a note to SPEC.md: adding a state is free precisely because the
  value is a string, and renaming one is expensive for the same reason. Readable
  string enums trade a free schema for a data migration on rename.

  3. "slice" → "step". Your reasoning is right — step 10 is pure test code, step
  1.5 was a static mockup; calling those vertical slices overstated it. Renamed
  across README, CLAUDE.md, all four docs, every spec docstring, code comments,
  and the time-log labels. Left alone: git history, the transcripts, and
  migration 0002 — all historical records.

  I changed one word in your AI section for consistency (slices → steps in the
  sentence about splitting the work) since the term itself changed. Say if you'd
  rather it stayed.

  Pending

  The gate that's running started before the rename, so I'll re-run it after
  committing — though the rename touched only comments and prose, no selectors
  or behaviour.

  Then search, which is queued as step 9.5.

      235 +### Step 9.5 — Search by name                                 
      236 +**S: M** · **Spec:** `09-pagination-scale` (F4, F10, F11, F12)
          + · **Validation: T2 + index verification**                    
      237  
      238 +Cut from step 9 for time, then added back: at large job counts
          + it is the control that makes the list                        
      239 +usable, and the status filter is not a substitute — you cannot
          + find "the combustor run from Tuesday" with                   
      240 +five categorical chips.                                       
      241 +                                                              
      242 +It is not a text box. `name ILIKE '%term%'` has a leading wild
          +card, so a btree cannot serve it at all and                   
      243 +Postgres falls back to a sequential scan — the exact query sha
          +pe everything else here avoids. Migration                     
      244 +`0004_search_trgm` enables `pg_trgm` (which ships with the `po
          +stgres:16` image, so it is a migration                        
      245 +rather than a deployment prerequisite) and adds a GIN trigram 
          +index over `name`.                                            
      246 +                                                              
      247 +- server-side across the whole table, never over the loaded pa
          +ge — F10 buries the target under 40 newer                     
      248 +  jobs so a page-local implementation provably fails          
      249 +- debounced at 300ms, so a burst of typing is not a request pe
          +r keystroke (F4)                                              
      250 +- whitespace-only trimmed on both ends to an absent filter, no
          +t a filter matching nothing (F11)                             
      251 +- composes with the status filter as AND (F12)                
      252 +                                                              
      253 +The costs are named rather than hidden: a GIN index is larger 
          +than a btree and adds write amplification on                  
      254 +insert and on any name change, and a highly unselective term s
          +till has to sort a large candidate set.                       
      255 +Trigram makes substring search viable, not free.              
      256 +                                                              
      257 +*Consequence for the footer:* an exact "showing x of y" is now
          + firmly out, not merely unbuilt. A total for                  
      258 +a search result cannot come from status counters — it needs a 
          +`COUNT(*)` over a trigram match on every                      
      259 +keystroke, which is the hot-path count cursor pagination exist
          +s to avoid.

⏺ Update(docs/SPEC.md)
  ⎿  Added 21 lines, removed 10 lines
      211  never a silently empty result. `IN` over the leading column of
            `(current_status, created_at, id)`
      212  stays a set of index range seeks, so selecting four statuses c
           osts about what selecting one does.
      213  
      214 -**Search by name is designed and deliberately not built** (see
          - PLAN.md step 9). It is not a text box:                       
      215 -`name ILIKE '%combustor%'` **cannot use a btree index** — a le
          -ading wildcard forces a sequential scan,                      
      216 -exactly the query shape that falls over on a multi-million-row
          - table. Doing it honestly needs a trigram                     
      217 -index:                                                        
      214 +`search` matches anywhere in the name, case-insensitively, and
          + is applied the same way — server-side over                   
      215 +the whole table. It is **not** a text box over the loaded rows
          +: at this scale the match is usually not on                   
      216 +the page already fetched, so narrowing client-side returns not
          +hing and the user concludes the job does not                  
      217 +exist.                                                        
      218  
      219 +`name ILIKE '%combustor%'` **cannot use a btree index** — a le
          +ading wildcard leaves no prefix to seek on,                   
      220 +so Postgres falls back to a sequential scan, the one query sha
          +pe this design avoids everywhere else. So                     
      221 +the feature comes with an index built for it (`0004_search_trg
          +m`):                                                          
      222 +                                                              
      223  ```sql
      224  CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pg_trgm;
      225  CREATE INDEX job_name_trgm_idx ON jobs_job USING gin (name gin
           _trgm_ops);
      226  ```
      227  
      224 -`pg_trgm` ships with the official `postgres:16` image, so it i
          -s a migration (`CreateExtension` +                            
      225 -`AddIndex`) rather than a deployment prerequisite — and stated
          - honestly, a *highly unselective* term still                  
      226 -has to sort a large candidate set, so trigram makes substring 
          -search viable rather than free. It was cut                    
      227 -because the assignment does not ask for it and an unindexed ve
          -rsion would contradict the very performance                   
      228 -claim the scale step exists to make. The status filter already
          - demonstrates the server-side-narrowing                       
      229 -property.                                                     
      228 +`pg_trgm` ships with the official `postgres:16` image, so this
          + is a migration rather than a deployment                      
      229 +prerequisite — which is the whole reason it is affordable here
          +.                                                             
      230  
      231 +Stated honestly, the costs: a GIN index is larger than a btree
          + and adds write amplification on every                        
      232 +insert and every name change, and a *highly unselective* term

          +171.2 ms | Bitmap Index Scan on `job_name_trgm_idx` |

          +Index Scan on `job_created_desc_idx`, filtering |             
      286 +                                                              
      287 +The planner picks a *different* index for each, and both beat 
          +the sequential scan by a wide margin —                        
      288 +but for opposite reasons. A **rare** term means the trigram in
          +dex earns its place: nothing else can find                    
      289 +one row in a quarter of a million without reading them all. A 
          +**common** term means the trigram index is                    
      290 +not even used — walking the existing `created_at` ordering ind
          +ex and filtering satisfies `LIMIT 25` after a                 
      291 +few dozen rows, long before a bitmap over 17,800 matches would

⏺ Search is built and the gate is running. The measurement turned up something
  worth your attention.

  Search works, and it's indexed for the query it actually runs

  - Migration 0004_search_trgm — TrigramExtension() then a GIN index with
  gin_trgm_ops on name. Verified live: extension present, job_name_trgm_idx
  created.
  - Backend — ?search= → name__icontains, whitespace trimmed to "no filter" on
  both ends.
  - Frontend — a search box in the toolbar, debounced 300 ms via a new
  useDebounced hook. The field updates instantly; only the query waits, which is
  the part usually got wrong.
  - 4 new specs — F10 (buries the target under 40 newer jobs so a page-local
  implementation provably fails), F4 (debounce), whitespace-only, and search
  composing with the status filter as AND. 13/13 in that file.

  The measurement was not the story I expected

  ┌──────────────┬─────────┬─────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Term     │ Indexed │ Index   │                 Plan                 │
  │              │         │   off   │                                      │
  ├──────────────┼─────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 1 match      │ 39.7 ms │ 171.2   │ Bitmap Index Scan on                 │
  │              │         │ ms      │ job_name_trgm_idx                    │
  ├──────────────┼─────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ~17,800      │ 0.42 ms │ 163.2   │ Index Scan on job_created_desc_idx,  │
  │ matches      │         │ ms      │ filtering                            │
  └──────────────┴─────────┴─────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘

  The planner picks a different index for each, and the unselective term —
  trigram's supposed weakness — is the fast one. That's pagination, not the
  index: the query only ever needs the first 25 matches, so a common term hands
  them over after a few dozen rows, long before a bitmap over 17,800 matches
  would finish.

  The selective term is the slower of the two, because a rare match forces most
  of the GIN index to be read. Still 4× faster than the scan it replaces, and
  it's the case that would otherwise be unusable.

  I wrote that up as measured rather than smoothing it into "index made it
  faster" — the nuance is the interesting part, and it's the kind of thing worth
  being able to explain out loud.

  One consequence recorded

  "Showing x of y" has moved from unbuilt to foreclosed. No counter can answer
  "how many names match %combustor%", so an exact total would need a COUNT(*)
  over a trigram match on every keystroke. The README now frames that as a
  decision search made, not one taken alongside it — and argues search is the
  more useful half, since a total tells you how much you didn't look at while
  search gets you to the row you wanted.

  Docs updated throughout: F4/F10 un-struck plus new F11/F12, PLAN gains step
  9.5, SPEC and README rewritten from "designed, not built" to built with costs
  named. No stale not-built claims left.

  I'll report the gate and commit when it lands.

  Two things worth knowing

  The search measurement contradicted my expectation, and I kept the honest 
  version. At 250k rows the planner picks a different index depending on the
  term: trigram for a rare one (39.7 ms vs 171 ms unindexed), and the plain
  created_at index for a common one (0.42 ms) — because LIMIT 25 is satisfied
  within a few dozen rows, long before a bitmap over ~17,800 matches would
  finish. So trigram's supposed weakness is the fast case here, thanks to
  pagination. That's in the README as measured, not smoothed into "index made it
  faster".

  Search foreclosed "showing x of y". It's no longer merely unbuilt — no counter
  can answer "how many names match %combustor%", so an exact total would need a
  COUNT(*) over a trigram match per keystroke. The README now frames that as a
  decision search made, and argues search is the better half of the pair.

  Only 206 jobs in the database now — make test reseeded to 30 plus fixtures, so
  the 250k is gone. Say the word if you want a large set back for feeling out
  search at scale; it's ~2 minutes.

      24  now_epoch() { date +%s; }
      25  now_iso()   { date -u +"%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ"; }
      26  
      27 +# Zone the *report* is rendered in. The ledger keeps UTC, which
         + is the right                                                  
      28 +# storage format — unambiguous and immune to DST. But rendering
         + UTC to a human                                                
      29 +# is actively misleading: a session worked on the evening of th
         +e 25th showed up                                               
      30 +# as "2026-07-26 00:09", wrong by a whole day. Override with RE
         +PORT_TZ.                                                       
      31 +REPORT_TZ="${REPORT_TZ:-America/Los_Angeles}"                  
      32 +                                                               
      33 +# Epoch -> "YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM" in REPORT_TZ.                    
      34 +#                                                              
      35 +# BSD date (macOS) reads an epoch with -r; GNU date wants -d @E
         +POCH, and treats                                               
      36 +# -r as "reference file" so it fails through to the fallback. T
         +he ledger stores                                               
      37 +# the epoch alongside the ISO string precisely so this never pa
         +rses a string                                                  
      38 +# back — which is the operation the two implementations disagre
         +e about.                                                       
      39 +local_stamp() {                                                
      40 +  TZ="$REPORT_TZ" date -r "$1" +"%Y-%m-%d %H:%M" 2>/dev/null \ 
      41 +    || TZ="$REPORT_TZ" date -d "@$1" +"%Y-%m-%d %H:%M"         
      42 +}                                                              
      43 +                                                               
      44 +zone_abbrev() {                                                
      45 +  TZ="$REPORT_TZ" date -r "$1" +%Z 2>/dev/null || TZ="$REPORT_T
         +Z" date -d "@$1" +%Z                                           
      46 +}

⏺ Done. The report now renders in PDT, and the fix was worth more than
  cosmetics:

  The dates were wrong, not just the hours

  The renderer sliced the UTC ISO string, so evening sessions landed on the
  following day. Your last session yesterday showed as 2026-07-26 00:09 when you
  worked at 17:09 on the 25th.

  Now:

  | 2026-07-25 | 08:08 | 08:33 | 0h 25m | planning …
  | 2026-07-25 | 17:09 | 17:24 | 0h 14m | code review fixes …
  | 2026-07-26 | 08:12 | 08:30 | 0h 18m | dialog copy, respelling, search …

  Yesterday's work all reads as the 25th; today's is correctly the 26th.

  The ledger still stores UTC — that's the right storage format, unambiguous and
  DST-proof. Only the rendering is local, and the report says so in its header,
  with the zone abbreviation printed from the actual date so it reads PDT or
  PST correctly depending on when it's rendered. Overridable via REPORT_TZ.

  The conversion reads from the epoch column rather than parsing the ISO string
  back, which is exactly why that column exists — string parsing is the
  operation BSD and GNU date disagree about. Handled both (date -r vs date -d 
  @), so it works on a grader's Linux box too.

  Two things I cleaned up while in there

  One label was misleading — it read step 10.6 … — step 9.5 … because I opened a
  timer for the dialog work and closed it with a note about search. Relabelled

         - and at a few

         - and a timeout.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)
⏺ That review earned its keep. Finding #1 was a real, shipped defect and I'd
  written a false claim about it in a graded deliverable.

  The bug

  Django compiles name__icontains to UPPER("jobs_job"."name"::text) LIKE 
  UPPER(…). I verified it:

  WHERE UPPER("jobs_job"."name"::text) LIKE UPPER(%combustor%)

  A gin_trgm_ops index on bare name cannot serve a predicate whose left side is 
  a function call. So migration 0004 was paying full GIN write amplification on
  every insert and rename while contributing nothing — and search at 250k rows
  was exactly the sequential scan the index existed to prevent.

  My measurement was on hand-written ILIKE, not on the ORM's query. That's why
  it looked fine. The index existed, EXPLAIN showed it being used, and none of
  it applied to the code path the app actually runs.

  The fix, and a second thing found while fixing it

  The index is now on UPPER(name::text). Getting there turned up that Django's
  own recipe is broken for this case — GinIndex(OpClass(Upper("name"), 
  name="gin_trgm_ops")) renders the operator class inside the expression parens:

  USING gin ((UPPER("name") gin_trgm_ops))   -- syntax error

  So 0004 uses explicit DDL, which both works and shows a reader precisely what
  was created. The Meta declaration is gone with a comment explaining why, and
  makemigrations --check reports no drift.

  Re-measured through QuerySet.explain(analyze=True) on the ORM's query, at 250k
  rows:

  ┌────────────┬─────────┬──────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │            │ Indexed │    Index     │               Plan               │
  │            │         │   disabled   │                                  │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 1 match    │ 30.6 ms │ 116.5 ms     │ Bitmap Index Scan on             │
  │            │         │              │ job_name_trgm_idx                │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ~18k       │ 0.304   │ 138.6 ms     │ Index Scan on                    │
  │ matches    │ ms      │              │ job_created_desc_idx             │
  └────────────┴─────────┴──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

  The qualitative story held, but every number was wrong. The README now states
  the method explicitly — through the ORM, not hand-written SQL — because that's
  the part that failed, not the arithmetic.

  The other seven

  - #3 "All" chip now tracks only the statuses it clears, instead of claiming to
  be clearable while a search was active.
  - #4 Search is trimmed before the query key, so "", "   " and "combustor "
  stop being three cache entries. That was a real full-list loading flash after
  typing one space.
  - #5 Empty-state copy names only the narrowings in effect.
  - #6 Mockup updated to match the shipped dialog — and it now shows both
  variants, with and without the cancel hint, since that conditional is the
  point.
  - #7 Debounce test rewritten: 20 chars over ~800 ms, so debounced and
  not-debounced differ by an order of magnitude rather than sitting on the
  window boundary. It now waits on an actual search response.
  - #8 Stale CANCELLED in a doc comment.

  Backend units and the full gate are running. I'll report before you push —
  this is not something to push unverified.

⏺ 5/5 in isolation — it only bites under parallel load. Reading the test,
  there's a genuine ordering hazard: after the failed delete, the rollback
  restores the row before the reconciling refetch completes, so the second
  attempt can start against a row that's still mid-settle and gets replaced
  under it.

  Fixing the ordering rather than bumping a timeout.

⏺ Gate running, with the full log kept this time rather than piped through tail.

  Status of the eight findings

  All fixed. The two that mattered:

  #1 was a genuine shipped defect — the index couldn't serve the query, and I'd
  published a measured claim about it that was taken on hand-written SQL. Now on
  UPPER(name::text), verified through QuerySet.explain() on the ORM's own
  query, with corrected numbers in the README.

  #4 was a real user-visible bug — typing one space after loading three pages
  blew the list away and refetched page 1 for a response already in cache.

  And I made one of my own along the way

  My fix for the flaky delete-recovery test introduced a failure. I asserted
  getByRole("button", { name: /^Delete$/ }), but that button's accessible name
  comes from aria-label="Delete <job name>", so an exact match can never resolve
  — the assertion failed against a perfectly healthy button, deterministically,
  three times out of three.

  Worth noting because it's the same shape of mistake as finding #1: verifying 
  against what I assumed the system emits rather than what it actually emits.
  Once on SQL, once on an accessible name. Both caught by running the thing
  instead of reasoning about it.

  The underlying flake was real, though, and the fix stands: after a failed
  delete the rollback restores the row before the reconciling refetch lands, so
  the second attempt could act on a node about to be detached. Now it waits for
  the Delete button to be enabled, which is the observable signal that
  deletingIds has cleared. 30/30 across repeats.

  Two things for the record

  The review also confirmed independently that migration 0003's data move was
  correct, both directions reverse cleanly, and the TEST_PLAN counts I
  recomputed (61 cases, 21 negative, 144 specs) are accurate.

  I'll write both reviews up in .review/ and commit once the gate reports. Not
  pushing until it's green — finding #1 is exactly the kind of thing that
  shouldn't leave the machine unverified.

✻ Cogitated for 24s

❯ I ran make test on another machine and it onlhy took a few minutes so it      
  could just be my machine?                                                     

⏺ Very likely, yes. Your Mac is 8 GB running Docker Desktop, which is a
  known-slow combination for image builds, and the biggest single cost — the
  ~100 MB Chromium download — is bandwidth-bound too.

  The 16 minutes I watched here was real but not representative: playwright 
  install --with-deps chromium alone took 473 seconds on this machine.

  One thing that would change the conclusion: had that machine ever pulled
  node:22-bookworm, python:3.12-slim-bookworm, postgres:16-alpine or built this
  project before? If those layers were already in its Docker cache, the run
  skipped the expensive part and "a few minutes" is a warm number, not a cold
  one. If it was genuinely a fresh Docker install, then the gate is simply fine
  and my estimate was hardware-specific.

  Either way, the decision is the same and it's the cheaper one:

  No scary warning in the README. "First run takes 15–20 minutes" would be wrong
  for most graders and might read as an excuse. I'd either say nothing, or add
  one neutral line to the testing section — "the first run builds three images 
  and downloads Chromium; subsequent runs are ~2 minutes" — which is true
  everywhere and sets expectations without alarming anyone. Your call; I lean
  toward the one line.

  This also unblocks the thing I was holding:

  Digest pinning is now safe to do — the only reason I held it was so your
  timing run measured a build I'd validated. That's done. It's five one-line
  changes plus verification on both architectures, ~20 minutes, and it makes T3
  mean what it claims: right now node:22-bookworm floats and moved under us
  mid-session, so a grader's cold build can pull a different image than the one
  you tested.

  Want me to do the pinning and the one-line README note, then the final T3 ×2?

✻ Cooked for 24s

❯ I'm actually running orbtask instead of docker desktop                        

⏺ Then scratch my Docker Desktop explanation — OrbStack is generally faster at
  the VM and filesystem layer, so that wasn't it.

  The better explanation for the 16 minutes here:

  1. A one-off base-image re-pull. node:22-bookworm moved mid-session, which
  invalidated the layer cache and forced playwright install --with-deps chromium
  to run again — 473 s on its own. A normal run doesn't hit that.
  2. The rest is network-bound, not CPU or runtime: apt dependencies plus a ~100
  MB browser download.

  So it was mostly bandwidth and bad luck, not your machine and not your
  container runtime. Which makes "a few minutes elsewhere" entirely consistent —
  and means the gate is fine as-is.

  That sharpens the case for pinning, and narrows what it actually buys:

  Digest pinning doesn't make the first build faster — a grader still downloads
  everything once. What it prevents is precisely what bit us: a tag moving
  underneath the build, so what a grader compiles is byte-identical to what you
  validated. CLAUDE.md claims every base image tag is pinned; by version they
  are, by digest they aren't, and T3's "cold verified" claim is only as strong
  as that.

  Nothing about this changes because you're on OrbStack — the project only
  requires docker and docker compose, which OrbStack, Docker Desktop, colima and
  plain Linux Docker all provide identically.

  So, unchanged plan and I'd like to get on with it:

  1. Digest pinning — five one-line changes, verify on arm64 and amd64 (~20
  min).
  2. One neutral README line — first run builds images and downloads Chromium,
  later runs ~2 minutes. True on any machine, no alarm.
  3. Final T3 ×2 — warm, then fully pruned.

  Shall I start on those?

✻ Crunched for 24s

❯ Actually when I just ran make test in my terminal, it only took around 8      
  minutes. I ran make clean the make test. I wonder if it's a ram issue with    
  all the other processes runnig                                                

⏺ Worth checking something concrete rather than guessing about RAM — how cold
  make clean actually leaves things.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Found something better than the RAM theory — and it matters for the project's
  central claim.

  make clean doesn't leave Docker cold

  clean: $(COMPOSE) down --volumes --remove-orphans --rmi local

  --rmi local removes the images this project built. It does not remove pulled
  base images, and it doesn't touch the BuildKit cache. Right now:

  Reclaimable: 896.1MB     (entries 26 hours old)

  So make clean && make test rebuilds from cached layers — including the
  expensive playwright install --with-deps chromium step. That's why it was 8
  minutes rather than 16, and it explains the difference without needing RAM:
  the 16-minute run had node:22-bookworm move underneath it, which invalidated
  the cache and forced a genuine re-download. The 8-minute run reused it.

  RAM pressure is real on 8 GB and won't have helped, but it wouldn't produce a
  clean 2× that correlates exactly with a base-image change.

  The part that actually matters

  docs/PLAN.md defines T3 as "make clean && make build && make test from pruned 
  Docker". The "from pruned Docker" is carrying the whole claim — and make clean
  doesn't prune. So every T3 run so far has been warmer than a grader's first
  run, which has:

  - no base images
  - no BuildKit cache

  That's exactly the class of gap T3 exists to catch, and it's sitting in T3
  itself. A truly cold run needs docker builder prune -af plus dropping the base
  images.

  What I'd do about it

  Add a make clean-all that genuinely prunes — build cache and base images — and
  make T3's definition name that command instead of make clean. Keep make clean
  as the fast day-to-day reset it already is.

  That way "cold verified" means what it says, and it's the one claim the whole
  gate story rests on.

  Roughly 15 minutes, and it should land before the final T3 so that run is the
  real thing. Want me to do that along with the digest pinning?

  first one that has actually been cold. Meanwhile, answers to the two questions
  in your list:

  postgres:16-alpine — yes, same problem, now pinned

  Every floating tag has it. All five are pinned by manifest-list digest, so
  they still resolve per-architecture and your arm64 Mac and the amd64 check
  both keep working:

  ┌───────────────────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │                           │                  │

  └───────────────────────────┴──────────────────┘

  postgres was arguably the most important one — a base image change there means
  a different Postgres patch level under your measurements.

  The database port is already gate-free

  docker-compose.yml publishes no ports at all — 8080, 8000 and 55432 are all in
  docker-compose.dev.yml, which only make up overlays. So the debug/gate split
  you're describing already exists and nothing needs removing. Your instinct was
  right; it was just implemented earlier.

  Everything else

  - make clean-all — removes the pulled base images and prunes the BuildKit
  cache. It derives the image list from the Dockerfiles rather than hardcoding,
  so it stays correct. T3 and case A1 now name this command; make clean stays
  the fast day-to-day reset.
  - shm_size: 1gb on db and e2e. For Postgres it removes planner variance from
  shared-memory pressure during the 250k work. For the e2e container it's the
  classic Chromium "Target crashed" cause — the usual workaround is
  --disable-dev-shm-usage, which changes the browser to suit the harness; giving
  it memory keeps the browser under test the same one a user runs.
  - Transcripts renamed ai-session-transcript-0…3-… so they list oldest-first.
  - Reviews moved to docs/reviews/, un-ignored, with an index README framing
  them as part of the process. The "not a deliverable" note is gone from each.

  work and the job list, and step 7's 40 minutes was largely T3 validation of

  three review rounds rather than two.

      116 -### Data model                                                
       78 +### The one idea worth understanding                          
       79  
      118 -`JobStatus` is an **append-only event log** and the source of 
          -truth. `Job.current_status` and                               
      119 -`Job.current_status_at` are a **projection** of that log, writ
          -ten only by                                                   
      120 -`services.record_status()` inside the same transaction as the 
          -event.                                                        
       80 +`JobStatus` is an **append-only event log**. `Job.current_stat
          +us` / `current_status_at` are a                               
       81 +**projection** of it, written only by `services.record_status(
          +)`, in the same transaction as the                            
       82 +event.                                                        
       83  
      122 -```mermaid                                                    
      123 -erDiagram                                                     
      124 -    JOB ||--o{ JOB_STATUS : "has many (cascade)"              
      125 -    JOB {                                                     
      126 -        bigint   id PK                                        
      127 -        varchar  name                                         
      128 -        datetime created_at                                   
      129 -        datetime updated_at                                   
      130 -        varchar  current_status "PROJECTION"                  
      131 -        datetime current_status_at "timestamp of the event beh
          -ind it"                                                       
      132 -    }                                                         
      133 -    JOB_STATUS {                                              
      134 -        bigint   id PK                                        
      135 -        bigint   job_id FK                                    
      136 -        varchar  status_type "PENDING | RUNNING | COMPLETED | 
          -FAILED | CANCELED"                                            
      137 -        datetime timestamp "server-stamped"                   
      138 -    }                                                         
      139 -```                                                           
       84 +The projection exists for one query: `?status=RUNNING`. Derivi
          +ng status on read is perfectly                                
       85 +normalized and fine for an unfiltered page, but *filtering* by
          + it would make Postgres compute the                           
       86 +latest status for every job before applying the predicate — a 
          +full scan no index can help.                                  
       87  
      141 -The projection exists for one query in particular: `?status=RU
          -NNING`. Deriving status on read is                            
      142 -perfectly normalized and fine for an unfiltered page, but *fil
          -tering* by it would make Postgres                             
      143 -compute the latest status for every job in the table before ap
          -plying the predicate — a full scan                            
      144 -no index can help with.                                       
       88 +The event is appended unconditionally; only the projection is 
          +conditional. That is what makes the                           
       89 +write safe to replay, and keeps the log authoritative if the p
          +rojection ever has to be rebuilt.                             
       90  
       91  ### Status transitions
       92  
      148 -Enforced by `jobs/transitions.py` and mirrored in the UI, so a
          -n illegal move is *unreachable*                               
      149 -rather than merely rejected.                                  
       93 +Enforced in `jobs/transitions.py` and mirrored in the UI from 
          +the API's `allowed_transitions`, so                           
       94 +an illegal move is *unreachable* rather than merely rejected —
          + and the state machine is never                               
       95 +duplicated in TypeScript.                                     
       96  
       97  ```
       98  PENDING   → RUNNING, FAILED, CANCELED
     ...
      102  CANCELED  → ∅  + Re-run → PENDING      terminal, retryable
      103  ```
      104  
      159 -You retry work that did not finish. Re-running something that 
          -*succeeded* is a new job, not a                               
      160 -retry — so `COMPLETED` is a genuine dead end.                 
      105 +You retry work that did not finish; re-running something that 
          +*succeeded* is a new job.                                     
      106 +**Canceling is not deleting** — a canceled job still consumed 
          +compute time, and that history is                             
      107 +worth auditing.                                               
      108  
      162 -**Canceling is not deleting.** Delete removes the record and c
          -ascades the log away; cancel stops                            
      163 -the work and keeps both. A canceled job still consumed compute
          - time, and that is exactly the                                
      164 -history worth auditing.

          +tatus=` (repeatable, OR-ed) and `?search=` apply server-side a

      191 -## Performance considerations                                 
      131 +## Performance                                                
      132  
      193 -The brief asks what happens with **millions of jobs in the dat
          -abase**. The working assumption:                              
      194 -the dataset is large, a single user's viewport is not. Nobody 
          -scrolls a million rows. So the                                
      195 -engineering problem is making every query cost proportional to
          - the **page**, not the **table**.                             
      133 +The brief asks about **millions of jobs**. Working assumption:
          + the dataset is large, a single                               
      134 +user's viewport is not — so every query costs proportional to 
          +the **page**, not the table.                                  
      135  
      197 -**Cursor (keyset) pagination, never offset.** Two things break
          - page-number pagination at scale,                             
      198 -and cursor pagination avoids both:                            
      136 +**Cursor pagination, never offset**, with composite indexes co
          +vering the exact `ORDER BY` and                               
      137 +`WHERE`. Measured at 250,000 rows, same page, same data:      
      138  
      200 -- `OFFSET 500000` makes Postgres walk and discard half a milli
          -on rows before returning anything.                            
      201 -  A keyset predicate — `WHERE (created_at, id) < (…)` — is an 
          -index seek, so page 20,000 costs                              
      202 -  what page 1 costs.                                          
      203 -- DRF's `PageNumberPagination` issues a `COUNT(*)` on **every*
          -* request to build the `count`                                
      204 -  field. On a multi-million-row table that is a full index sca
          -n per page load, on the hot path,                             
      205 -  for a number nobody reads. Cursor pagination has none.      
      206 -                                                              
      207 -The trade-off, stated plainly: no "jump to page 47". For a fee
          -d-shaped dashboard that is the                                
      208 -right trade — nobody navigates a large list by page number; th
          -ey narrow it.                                                 
      209 -                                                              
      210 -### Measured, not asserted                                    
      211 -                                                              
      212 -Seeded to **250,000 jobs** (`make seed N=250000`, ~1m50s) with
          - roughly 640,000 status rows behind                           
      213 -them. Query cost from `EXPLAIN ANALYZE`, on the same table, fo
          -r the same 25 rows 200,000 deep:                              
      214 -                                                              
      215 -| Query | Execution time |                                    
      139 +| Query | Time |                                              
      140  |---|---|
      217 -| Keyset seek — `WHERE created_at < … ORDER BY … LIMIT 25` | *
          -*0.101 ms** |                                                 
      218 -| `OFFSET 200000 LIMIT 25` — the same page, the other way | **
          -39.703 ms** |                                                 
      219 -| `COUNT(*)` — what `PageNumberPagination` adds to *every* req
          -uest | **30.557 ms** |                                        
      141 +| Keyset seek | **0.101 ms** |                                
      142 +| `OFFSET 200000 LIMIT 25` — the same page, the other way | 39
          +.7 ms |                                                       
      143 +| `COUNT(*)` — what `PageNumberPagination` adds to *every* req
          +uest | 30.6 ms |                                              
      144  
      221 -The keyset seek is **~390× faster** than the offset it replace
          -s, and the count DRF would have run                           
      222 -on every page load costs more on its own than serving the page
          -. Neither number is a guess about                             
      223 -what happens at a million rows; both are measured at a quarter
          - of one, and the shapes are what                              
      224 -matter — the seek is flat in depth, the other two are linear i
          -n table size.                                                 
      145 +~390× on the seek, and the count DRF would run per page load c
          +osts more than serving the page.                              
      146  
      226 -End to end over HTTP, including Django and serialization:     
      147 +Search is indexed with a GIN trigram index on `UPPER(name::tex
          +t)` — the expression Django actually                          
      148 +emits for `icontains`, which matters more than it sounds.     
      149  
      228 -| Rows in table | First page | 200,000 rows deep | Filtered by
          - status |                                                     
      229 -|---|---|---|---|                                             
      230 -| 100 | 10.6 ms | 10.0 ms | 10.1 ms |                         
      231 -| 250,000 | 19.0 ms | 28.3 ms | 16.7 ms |                     
      150 +**Full writeup, including three features deliberately not buil
          +t and why:                                                    
      151 +[docs/PERFORMANCE.md](docs/PERFORMANCE.md)**                  
      152  
      233 -Medians of 15 samples. The 2,500× increase in table size costs
          - roughly 8 ms, and walking 200,000                            
      234 -rows in costs about 9 ms more than the first page — the depth-
          -independence the design claims,                               
      235 -rather than a promise about it. The residual difference is buf
          -fer-cache behaviour on a larger                               
      236 -index, not the pagination strategy.                           
      237 -                                                              
      238 -**Virtualization: judged unnecessary, not overlooked.** Server
          - cost is flat, but DOM cost is linear                         
      239 -in what the user has *loaded* — and loading is an explicit "Lo
          -ad more" click of 25 rows, not                                
      240 -infinite scroll. Reaching even 500 rendered rows takes 20 deli
          -berate actions. Virtualizing would                            
      241 -add a dependency and complicate every spec that addresses rows
          - by selector, to fix a cost this UI                           
      242 -does not reach. If load-more became infinite scroll, or the pa
          -ge size grew, the threshold worth                             
      243 -acting on is somewhere near 1,000 rendered rows.              
      244 -                                                              
      245 -**Composite indexes covering the exact `ORDER BY` and `WHERE`:
          -**                                                            
      246 -                                                              
      247 -| Index | Serves |                                            
      248 -|---|---|                                                     
      249 -| `Job (created_at DESC, id DESC)` | the default keyset walk |
      250 -| `Job (current_status, created_at DESC, id DESC)` | `?status=
          -` without a scan |                                            
      251 -| `JobStatus (job_id, timestamp DESC, id DESC)` | history look
          -ups, and a cheap cascade delete |                             
      252 -                                                              
      253 -`id` is the tiebreaker everywhere so the ordering is **total**
          -. Without it, two rows sharing a                              
      254 -timestamp can be skipped or repeated across page boundaries.  
      255 -                                                              
      256 -**The status filter runs server-side, across the whole table**
          - — never over the loaded page. Narrowing                      
      257 -client-side would mean a job that matches on page 400 simply d
          -oes not appear and the user concludes it                      
      258 -does not exist: a wrong answer delivered quickly. The filter i
          -s repeatable and OR-ed                                        
      259 -(`?status=RUNNING&status=FAILED`), and `IN` over the leading c
          -olumn of the composite index stays a set                      
      260 -of range seeks, so selecting four statuses costs about what se
          -lecting one does.                                             
      261 -                                                              
      262 -**Search by name is indexed for the query it actually runs.** 
          -`name ILIKE '%combustor%'` **cannot use a                     
      263 -btree index**: a leading wildcard leaves no prefix to seek on,
          - so Postgres falls back to a sequential scan                  
      264 -— precisely the shape that falls over at the scale this sectio
          -n is about. So search ships with a GIN                        
      265 -trigram index built for it (`0004_search_trgm`), and `pg_trgm`
          - comes with the `postgres:16` image, making                   
      266 -it a migration rather than a deployment prerequisite.         
      267 -                                                              
      268 -It was cut for time earlier in the build and added back, becau
          -se at large job counts it is the control                      
      269 -that makes the list usable — five categorical chips cannot fin
          -d "the combustor run from Tuesday". The                       
      270 -version that shipped is the indexed one; an unindexed substrin
          -g scan would have contradicted the very                       
      271 -claim this section makes.                                     
      272 -                                                              
      273 -Debounced at 300ms, so a burst of typing is one query rather t
          -han one per keystroke, each of which would                    
      274 -be a trigram scan the next character immediately makes irrelev
          -ant.                                                          
      275 -                                                              
      276 -The costs, named rather than hidden: a GIN index is larger tha
          -n a btree and adds write amplification on                     
      277 -every insert and every name change, and a highly unselective t
          -erm still has to sort a large candidate set.                  
      278 -Trigram makes substring search viable, not free.              
      279 -                                                              
      280 -**The index is on `UPPER(name::text)`, not on `name`** — becau
          -se that is what Django compiles                               
      281 -`name__icontains` to on PostgreSQL. An index on the bare colum
          -n cannot serve a predicate whose left side                    
      282 -is a function call: it would exist, look correct, pay the full
          - GIN write cost on every insert and rename,                   
      283 -and leave the search as the sequential scan it was meant to pr
          -event. Quiet, expensive, and easy to ship.                    
      284 -                                                              
      285 -**Measured at 250,000 rows, through the ORM's own query** — `Q
          -uerySet.explain(analyze=True)` rather than                    
      286 -hand-written SQL, which is the only way to know the applicatio
          -n's query uses the index rather than one that                 
      287 -merely resembles it:                                          
      288 -                                                              
      289 -| Term | Indexed | Index disabled | Plan chosen |             
      290 -|---|---|---|---|                                             
      291 -| `Combustion Optimization #1234` — 1 match | **30.6 ms** | 11
          -6.5 ms | Bitmap Index Scan on `job_name_trgm_idx` |           
      292 -| `Combustion` — ~18,000 matches | **0.304 ms** | 138.6 ms | I
          -ndex Scan on `job_created_desc_idx`, filtering |              
      293 -                                                              
      294 -The planner picks a *different* index for each, and both beat 
          -the sequential scan by a wide margin — but                    
      295 -for opposite reasons. A **rare** term is where the trigram ind
          -ex earns its place: nothing else finds one                    
      296 -row in a quarter of a million without reading them all. A **co
          -mmon** term does not use the trigram index at                 
      297 -all — walking the existing `created_at` ordering index and fil
          -tering satisfies `LIMIT 25` within a few                      
      298 -dozen rows, long before a bitmap over 18,000 matches would hav
          -e finished.                                                   
      299 -                                                              
      300 -So the unselective case, usually cited as trigram's weakness, 
          -is the fast one here. That is a consequence                   
      301 -of pagination rather than of the index: the query only ever ne
          -eds the first 25 matches, and common terms                    
      302 -hand those over immediately. The selective case is the slower 
          -of the two at 30.6 ms, because a rare term                    
      303 -forces most of the GIN index to be read — still ~4× faster tha
          -n the scan it replaces, and the case that                     
      304 -would otherwise be unusable.                                  
      305 -                                                              
      306 -**Sorting by column: declined, and the reason is the interesti
          -ng part.** It looks like the smallest                         
      307 -of the features left out and is actually the largest, because 
          -the cursor encodes a position *in a                           
      308 -specific ordering*. Three consequences:                       
      309 -                                                              
      310 -- Every sortable column needs its **own composite index** endi
          -ng in `-id`, or sorting is a full sort                        
      311 -  of the table — the precise cost this section exists to avoid
          -.                                                             
      312 -- Changing sort has to **reset the cursor**. A position in one
          - ordering is meaningless in another, so                       
      313 -  "sort by name" cannot preserve where you were.              
      314 -- **Sorting by status would be incorrect**, not merely slow. P
          -er the limitation recorded below, DRF                         
      315 -  builds its cursor predicate from `ordering[0]` alone and pag
          -es rows sharing that value by integer                         
      316 -  offset. Ordering by `created_at`, a collision needs two inse
          -rts in the same microsecond. Ordering by                      
      317 -  `current_status` there are **five distinct values in the ent
          -ire table**, so every page boundary                           
      318 -  falls inside a tie group and the offset path becomes the onl
          -y path — rows get skipped and repeated                        
      319 -  during an ordinary walk.                                    
      320 -                                                              
      321 -Sorting by `name` or a timestamp is a migration and an index a
          -way. Sorting by status requires the                           
      322 -composite-cursor subclass described below first. Offering the 
          -first and quietly omitting the second                         
      323 -would have been the worst option: a control that works on four
          - columns and silently corrupts                                
      324 -pagination on the fifth.                                      
      325 -                                                              
      326 -**No N+1.** `current_status` is a column, so listing jobs touc
          -hes one table. History is fetched                             
      327 -only when a row is expanded.                                  
      328 -                                                              
      329 -**Known limits, not hidden.** Deleting a row inside a `created
          -_at` tie group can still skip a row                           
      330 -during a paginated walk: DRF's cursor is built from `ordering[
          -0]` alone and pages ties with an                              
      331 -integer offset, so the `id` tiebreaker never reaches the curso
          -r itself. Collisions require two                              
      332 -inserts in the same microsecond. The fix, if it ever mattered,
          - is a `CursorPagination` subclass                             
      333 -encoding a composite position.                                
      334 -                                                              
      335 -**Described but not built** — honestly out of scope for a few-
          -hour build: read replicas, a                                  
      336 -caching layer, `JobStatus` partitioned by time, approximate co
          -unts from `pg_class.reltuples`.                               
      337 -                                                              
      338 -### Why "showing 25 of 1,204" is not in the footer            
      339 -                                                              
      340 -The footer reports what is loaded, never a total, and that is 
          -a consequence of cursor pagination                            
      341 -rather than an oversight — a total means `COUNT(*)` on the hot
          - path, which is the second of the two                         
      342 -reasons cursor pagination was chosen at all.                  
      343 -                                                              
      344 -A total *can* be had cheaply, but only while filtering stays c
          -ategorical. With per-status counters                          
      345 -maintained in `record_status()`, the total for any status sele
          -ction is arithmetic over five rows —                          
      346 -no count, no scan. That design is written up under            
      347 -[counts by status](#counts-by-status--designed-deliberately-no
          -t-built).                                                     
      348 -                                                              
      349 -That stopped being available the moment search shipped. No cou
          -nter can answer "how many names match                         
      350 -`%combustor%`"; that needs a real `COUNT(*)` over a trigram ma
          -tch, on every keystroke, on the hot path.                     
      351 -The escapes are a capped count ("25 of 1000+") or an approxima
          -tion from `pg_class.reltuples`, and both are                  
      352 -less honest than the number the footer shows now.             
      353 -                                                              
      354 -So this is a decision search *made*, not one taken alongside i
          -t. The two pull against each other and                        
      355 -search is the more useful half: a total tells you how much you
          - did not look at, while search gets you to                    
      356 -the row you wanted. Given one, the footer reporting what is lo
          -aded is the truthful option.                                  
      357 -                                                              
      358 -### Counts by status — designed, deliberately not built       
      359 -                                                              
      360 -A dashboard wants a total and a per-status breakdown. The obvi
          -ous implementation is the one this                            
      361 -design specifically rules out: `SELECT current_status, COUNT(*
          -) … GROUP BY current_status` on                               
      362 -every page load is a scan of the whole table for a number that
          - changes by one at a time, on the                             
      363 -hot path, exactly like the `COUNT(*)` that cursor pagination e
          -xists to avoid.                                               
      364 -                                                              
      365 -The answer is to **maintain the counts on write instead of der
          -iving them on read**, and the                                 
      366 -architecture already has the one thing that makes it cheap: `s
          -ervices.record_status()` is the                               
      367 -sole writer of the projection, so the counter moves inside the
          - transaction that is already open                             
      368 -and already holds the job's row lock.                         
      369 -                                                              
      370 -| Path | Effect on the counters |                             
      371 -|---|---|                                                     
      372 -| `create_job()` | `PENDING` +1 |                             
      373 -| `record_status()`, when the projection advances | old −1, ne
          -w +1 |                                                        
      374 -| delete | current −1 |                                       
      375 -| `seed_jobs` | tallies each batch it builds and applies one u
          -pdate per batch |                                             
      376 -| `seed_jobs --clear` | resets to zero |                      
      377 -                                                              
      378 -Note the seed row. It bypasses `record_status()` by design — 2
          -50k jobs cannot be a quarter of a                             
      379 -million round trips — so the naive version of this feature dri
          -fts every time the database is                                
      380 -seeded. It tallies instead, which costs about four lines and k
          -eeps the counts exact on every path                           
      381 -the application actually uses.                                
      382 -                                                              
      383 -Two things would still be true, and both are the interesting p
          -art of the conversation rather than                           
      384 -the code:                                                     
      385 -                                                              
      386 -- **Drift is possible, just not from the app.** Raw SQL or a s
          -hell session that writes around the                           
      387 -  services can desynchronize the counters. Production wants a 
          -periodic reconciler recomputing from                          
      388 -  the projection and reporting the delta — cheap to write, and
          - its output is a genuine health                               
      389 -  signal, since a non-zero delta means something is writing ou
          -tside the service layer.                                      
      390 -- **One row per status is a hot row.** Every concurrent transi
          -tion into `RUNNING` serializes on                             
      391 -  the same counter row. Fine here, a real bottleneck at write 
          -volume; the standard fix is a                                 
      392 -  sharded counter — *N* rows per status, summed on read — trad
          -ing a slightly more expensive read                            
      393 -  for contention that scales.                                 
      394 -                                                              
      395 -The same reconciler answers a second question this design leav
          -es open: **orphaned `JobStatus`                               
      396 -rows.** The cascade is Django's ORM collector rather than an `
          -ON DELETE CASCADE` constraint, which                          
      397 -is sufficient while every write goes through the ORM but leave
          -s nothing at the database level to                            
      398 -prevent orphans if a delete fails partway. A sweeper reaping s
          -tatus rows whose job no longer                                
      399 -exists covers it, and is the same shape of job.               
      400 -                                                              
      401 -Left out because the assignment is a few hours and this is a s
          -ystems-design discussion, not a                               
      402 -requirement — but the design is settled rather than hand-waved
          -, which is why it is written down                             
      403 -here.                                                         
      404 -

          - passes warm and fails cold.

      450 -Recovery is the assertion that matters most: an app that repor
          -ts a failure but can never move past it                       
      451 -has handled the error only in the sense that it did not crash.
          - So every fault is injected **once** —                        
      452 -the second attempt has to succeed, and the banner has to go wi
          -th it.

      198  ## Design decisions
      199  
      200  Recorded with their reasoning in [`docs/OPEN_QUESTIONS.md`](do
           cs/OPEN_QUESTIONS.md) — what was
      463 -asked, what was decided, and what was assumed without asking. 
          -A few worth surfacing:                                        
      201 +asked, what was decided, and what was assumed without asking. 
          +Four worth surfacing:                                         
      202  
      465 -- **The projection guard compares against `current_status_at`,
          - never `updated_at`.** Any save                               
      466 -  bumps `updated_at` — including a rename — so guarding on it 
          -would let renaming a job silently                             
      467 -  discard a later status event.                               
      203 +- **The projection guard compares `current_status_at`, never `
          +updated_at`.** Any save bumps                                 
      204 +  `updated_at` — including a rename — so guarding on it would 
          +let renaming a job silently discard a                         
      205 +  later status event.                                         
      206  - **Sequential bigint ids, kept for readability.** "Job 328491
           7 is stuck" is usable in a support
      207    ticket; a UUID is not. The trade-off is enumerability, which
            matters once auth exists — and the
      208    answer then is UUIDv7, not v4.
      471 -- **Status is stored as text, not an int or a native Postgres 
          -`ENUM`.** Readable in psql, in                                
      472 -  logs, and on the wire; adding `CANCELED` was a genuine no-op
          - migration.                                                   
      209 +- **Status stored as text**, not an int or a native `ENUM`. Re
          +adable in psql, in logs, and on the                           
      210 +  wire; adding `CANCELED` was a no-op migration. Renaming it l
          +ater cost a data migration, which is                          
      211 +  the same coin.                                              
      212  - **No authentication, no multi-tenancy.** The brief never int
           roduces a user concept.
